People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Well, I came in because... things at school have just been feeling very pointed lately. I can’t shake the feeling that certain staff emails and the morning announcements are actually coded messages about me. Which, you know, the announcements are so loud anyway because the PA speaker in my classroom has this awful buzz. I told Mr. Henderson—he's our head custodian, always wears these bright yellow suspenders, just a lovely man—I told him it sounds like a hornet's nest in there. He said he’d put in a work order, but with the district budget cuts, who knows. They slashed the arts funding completely this year, and my friend Susan had to buy all her own papier-mâché supplies out of pocket. It's just a crying shame how they treat the arts in public education. 

But anyway, my colleagues... they try to be helpful about the messages. They'll pull up their computer screens and gently show me that those emails were actually sent to everyone on the main staff list, not just me. But I still... I don't know, I just keep thinking about how Susan's classroom always smells like wet newspaper now. Have you ever done papier-mâché? It takes absolutely forever to dry.

Mood changes? No, I wouldn't say so. I haven't been feeling overly energetic or depressed or anything. My sleep is pretty much the same as always. Well, except when the neighbor's dog starts barking at all hours. They have this little terrier mix, and it just yaps at absolutely nothing. I tried talking to them about it, but they just put up this new privacy fence instead of bringing the dog inside. Cedar, I think it is. It smells wonderful, actually, especially after it rains. I do love the smell of cedar. It reminds me of this old cedar chest my grandmother used to keep her winter quilts in. She had this beautiful patchwork one that she made completely by hand using scraps from old dresses. People just don't sew like that anymore, do they? It's all fast fashion now. You buy a sweater and it falls apart in the wash after two weeks. 

But anyway, no, I haven't been feeling down or unusually happy or anything like that. Just... just my normal self, really.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's definitely an actual voice. It's out loud. Usually it happens when things get really quiet, like when I'm home alone, or after the bell rings and the classroom finally empties out. I'll just be sitting there grading papers, and I'll hear this woman's voice. She'll say my name, or she'll make some little comment about what I'm doing, like, "Oh, you're marking that one wrong?" It's so clear that I actually answer her out loud before I even realize there's nobody else in the room with me. 

Speaking of grading, the new red pens the district ordered are just terrible. They bleed right through the paper. I don't know why they switched from the Pilot G2s. Those were perfect. They had a nice, smooth glide, and the ink never smeared. I actually wrote a very polite email to the purchasing department about it, but they never wrote back. I suppose they're too busy dealing with the fallout from the cafeteria vendor change. The new vendor serves these square pizzas that look exactly like the ones we had in the eighties. Do you remember those? With the little diced pepperonis that would crisp up into tiny bowls of grease? The kids absolutely hate them, but honestly, I think it's a bit nostalgic. I used to love pizza day when I was in middle school. We'd always trade our chocolate milks for extra tater tots. But anyway, yes, the voice is very clear, just like a real person standing right next to me.

No, I haven't been drinking or taking any drugs or anything like that. And my medications are exactly the same as they've always been. Just my daily vitamins, really. I take a multivitamin and some fish oil, though the fish oil capsules are so large they're terribly hard to swallow. I bought it at that new health food store that opened up where the old hardware store used to be. It's a shame about the hardware store. Mr. Henderson ran it for thirty years, and he always knew exactly which screw or nail you needed just by looking at what you were trying to fix. Now it's all organic quinoa and fifty-dollar jars of manuka honey. Have you ever tried manuka honey? They say it has antibacterial properties, but for that price, I'd rather just use Neosporin. I did buy a nice lavender soap there once, though. It had little sprigs of actual lavender in it, which looked pretty but made a terrible mess in the shower drain.

Well, I don't really think I have trouble staying on topic. People haven't said anything to me about it, anyway. I mean, I talk to my students all day, and they seem to follow along just fine. Most of them, anyway. You know, eighth graders can be so easily distracted. It was heartbreaking, really, because I had just made a huge batch of my grandmother's potato salad. She used to put a little bit of dill pickle relish in hers, which sounds strange, but it gives it this wonderful tang. I buy the relish in these giant jars from Costco. Have you been to Costco on a Saturday? It's absolute madness. People pushing those oversized carts like they're in a demolition derby. I nearly got run over in the bakery aisle by a woman with four rotisserie chickens. But anyway, no, I don't think I have any trouble staying focused when I'm talking to people.

I'll just be sitting there grading papers, and I'll hear this woman's voice. She'll say my name, or she'll make some little comment about what I'm doing, like, \"Oh, you're marking that one wrong?\" It's so clear that I actually answer her out loud before I even realize there's nobody else in the room with me. \n\nSpeaking of grading, the new red pens the district ordered are just terrible. They bleed right through the paper. I don't know why they switched from the Pilot G2s. Those were perfect. They had a nice, smooth glide, and the ink never smeared. I actually wrote a very polite email to the purchasing department about it, but they never wrote back. I suppose they're too busy dealing with the fallout from the cafeteria vendor change. The new vendor serves these square pizzas that look exactly like the ones we had in the eighties. Do you remember those? With the little diced pepperonis that would crisp up into tiny bowls of grease? The kids absolutely hate them, but honestly, I think it's a bit nostalgic. I used to love pizza day when I was in middle school. We'd always trade our chocolate milks for extra tater tots. But anyway, yes, the voice is very clear, just like a real person standing right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications during these past four months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been drinking or taking any drugs or anything like that. And my medications are exactly the same as they've always been. Just my daily vitamins, really. I take a multivitamin and some fish oil, though the fish oil capsules are so large they're terribly hard to swallow. I tried the liquid kind once, but it tasted exactly like you'd imagine—like a dock at low tide. It was awful. I bought it at that new health food store that opened up where the old hardware store used to be. It's a shame about the hardware store. Mr. Henderson ran it for thirty years, and he always knew exactly which screw or nail you needed just by looking at what you were trying to fix. Now it's all organic quinoa and fifty-dollar jars of manuka honey. Have you ever tried manuka honey? They say it has antibacterial properties, but for that price, I'd rather just use Neosporin. I did buy a nice lavender soap there once, though. It had little sprigs of actual lavender in it, which looked pretty but made a terrible mess in the shower drain. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no drugs or alcohol or new medicines at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that when you're talking with people, like we are now, you sometimes have trouble staying on one topic, or that others have told you it's hard to follow your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think I have trouble staying on topic. People haven't said anything to me about it, anyway. I mean, I talk to my students all day, and they seem to follow along just fine. Most of them, anyway. You know, eighth graders can be so easily distracted. Just yesterday, I was trying to explain the causes of the Civil War, and half the class was staring out the window because they were putting up a new flagpole in the courtyard. The old one got struck by lightning during that terrible storm we had last month. Did you lose power? We were out for three whole days. I had to throw away everything in my refrigerator. It was heartbreaking, really, because I had just made a huge batch of my grandmother's potato salad. She used to put a little bit of dill pickle relish in hers, which sounds strange, but it gives it this wonderful tang. I buy the relish in these giant jars from Costco. Have you been to Costco on a Saturday? It's absolute madness. People pushing those oversized carts like they're in a demolition derby. I nearly got run over in the bakery aisle by a woman with four rotisserie chickens. But anyway, no, I don't think I have any trouble staying focused when I'm talking to people.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these clearer symptoms started around the bake sale four months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like feeling more suspicious, withdrawing from friends or activities, or just feeling 'off' in a way others might have noticed?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, now that you mention it, I suppose things did start shifting a bit earlier. This whole change in how I think and work, and even how I connect with people... it's really been going on since my youngest left for college. That was about a year and two months ago. It was such an adjustment, having an empty house. I decided to take up knitting to fill the time, you know? I went to this lovely little yarn shop downtown, the one with the purple awning. They have the most exquisite merino wool. I bought three skeins of this gorgeous teal color because I thought I'd make a sweater, but then I realized I had no idea how to purl. The woman who owns the shop—I think her name is Beatrice, or maybe Bernadette—she offered to teach me, but her classes are on Tuesday nights, and Tuesday is when they air those wonderful British baking shows on PBS. Have you seen them? The things they can do with spun sugar are just incredible. I tried making a Victoria sponge cake once after watching an episode, but it came out completely flat. I think my baking powder was expired. It's so hard to keep track of expiration dates on things you only use once a year. I found a can of water chestnuts in the back of my pantry last week that expired in 2018! I just tossed the whole can out. Better safe than sorry, right? But yes, to answer your question, the changes started about fourteen months ago when my son moved out.

I actually wrote a very polite email to the purchasing department about it, but they never wrote back. I suppose they're too busy dealing with the fallout from the cafeteria vendor change. The new vendor serves these square pizzas that look exactly like the ones we had in the eighties. Do you remember those? With the little diced pepperonis that would crisp up into tiny bowls of grease? The kids absolutely hate them, but honestly, I think it's a bit nostalgic. I used to love pizza day when I was in middle school. We'd always trade our chocolate milks for extra tater tots. But anyway, yes, the voice is very clear, just like a real person standing right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications during these past four months?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't been drinking or taking any drugs or anything like that. And my medications are exactly the same as they've always been. Just my daily vitamins, really. I take a multivitamin and some fish oil, though the fish oil capsules are so large they're terribly hard to swallow. I tried the liquid kind once, but it tasted exactly like you'd imagine—like a dock at low tide. It was awful. I bought it at that new health food store that opened up where the old hardware store used to be. It's a shame about the hardware store. Mr. Henderson ran it for thirty years, and he always knew exactly which screw or nail you needed just by looking at what you were trying to fix. Now it's all organic quinoa and fifty-dollar jars of manuka honey. Have you ever tried manuka honey? They say it has antibacterial properties, but for that price, I'd rather just use Neosporin. I did buy a nice lavender soap there once, though. It had little sprigs of actual lavender in it, which looked pretty but made a terrible mess in the shower drain. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no drugs or alcohol or new medicines at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that when you're talking with people, like we are now, you sometimes have trouble staying on one topic, or that others have told you it's hard to follow your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really think I have trouble staying on topic. People haven't said anything to me about it, anyway. I mean, I talk to my students all day, and they seem to follow along just fine. Most of them, anyway. You know, eighth graders can be so easily distracted. Just yesterday, I was trying to explain the causes of the Civil War, and half the class was staring out the window because they were putting up a new flagpole in the courtyard. The old one got struck by lightning during that terrible storm we had last month. Did you lose power? We were out for three whole days. I had to throw away everything in my refrigerator. It was heartbreaking, really, because I had just made a huge batch of my grandmother's potato salad. She used to put a little bit of dill pickle relish in hers, which sounds strange, but it gives it this wonderful tang. I buy the relish in these giant jars from Costco. Have you been to Costco on a Saturday? It's absolute madness. People pushing those oversized carts like they're in a demolition derby. I nearly got run over in the bakery aisle by a woman with four rotisserie chickens. But anyway, no, I don't think I have any trouble staying focused when I'm talking to people.\n\nDoctor: Before these clearer symptoms started around the bake sale four months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like feeling more suspicious, withdrawing from friends or activities, or just feeling 'off' in a way others might have noticed?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you mention it, I suppose things did start shifting a bit earlier. This whole change in how I think and work, and even how I connect with people... it's really been going on since my youngest left for college. That was about a year and two months ago. It was such an adjustment, having an empty house. I decided to take up knitting to fill the time, you know? I went to this lovely little yarn shop downtown, the one with the purple awning. They have the most exquisite merino wool. I bought three skeins of this gorgeous teal color because I thought I'd make a sweater, but then I realized I had no idea how to purl. The woman who owns the shop—I think her name is Beatrice, or maybe Bernadette—she offered to teach me, but her classes are on Tuesday nights, and Tuesday is when they air those wonderful British baking shows on PBS. Have you seen them? The things they can do with spun sugar are just incredible. I tried making a Victoria sponge cake once after watching an episode, but it came out completely flat. I think my baking powder was expired. It's so hard to keep track of expiration dates on things you only use once a year. I found a can of water chestnuts in the back of my pantry last week that expired in 2018! I just tossed the whole can out. Better safe than sorry, right? But yes, to answer your question, the changes started about fourteen months ago when my son moved out."

Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. It's just a crying shame how they treat the arts in public education. \n\nBut anyway, my colleagues... they try to be helpful about the messages. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nMood changes? No, I wouldn't say so. I haven't been feeling overly energetic or depressed or anything. My sleep is pretty much the same as always. Well, except when the neighbor's dog starts barking at all hours. They have this little terrier mix, and it just yaps at absolutely nothing. I tried talking to them about it, but they just put up this new privacy fence instead of bringing the dog inside. Cedar, I think it is. It smells wonderful, actually, especially after it rains. I do love the smell of cedar. It reminds me of this old cedar chest my grandmother used to keep her winter quilts in. She had this beautiful patchwork one that she made completely by hand using scraps from old dresses. People just don't sew like that anymore, do they? It's all fast fashion now. You buy a sweater and it falls apart in the wash after two weeks. \n\nBut anyway, no, I haven't been feeling down or unusually happy or anything like that. Just... just my normal self, really.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\": \"None\", \"name\": \"None\", \"description\": \"None\", \"reason\": \"The doctor is asking about the use of alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications (substance use/medication history), which is not covered in the provided symptom profiles.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). It was heartbreaking, really, because I had just made a huge batch of my grandmother's potato salad. They have the most exquisite merino wool. I bought three skeins of this gorgeous teal color because I thought I'd make a sweater, but then I realized I had no idea how to purl. The woman who owns the shop—I think her name is Beatrice, or maybe Bernadette—she offered to teach me, but her classes are on Tuesday nights, and Tuesday is when they air those wonderful British baking shows on PBS. Have you seen them? The things they can do with spun sugar are just incredible. I tried making a Victoria sponge cake once after watching an episode, but it came out completely flat. I think my baking powder was expired. It's so hard to keep track of expiration dates on things you only use once a year. I found a can of water chestnuts in the back of my pantry last week that expired in 2018! I just tossed the whole can out. Better safe than sorry, right?
